Ǜी सतनाम िंसह संधू: आगे आने वाली बाढ़ इससे भी अिधक गंभीर हो सकती है। यह क ेवल एक 
Ģाकृितक आपदा नहीं है, बिÊक एक predictable disaster है, िजसे अगर समय रहते नहीं रोका 
गया, तो यह हर साल और अिधक िवकराल रूप लेती जाएगी। चेयरमैन सर, सबसे महत्वपूणर् बात 
यह है िक िपछली बाढ़ को 6 महीने बीत चुक
े  हȅ और अगली बाढ़ के मौसम मȂ अब केवल 4 महीने 
बाकी हȅ, लेिकन इतनी बड़ी तर्ासदी के बाद भी कोई action taken सामने नहीं आया है और न ही 
इसके  िलए responsible लोगȗ के िखलाफ कोई कारर्वाई की गई है। यिद हमने अभी भी 
proactive और preventive approach नहीं  अपनाई, तो
 यह तर्ासदी िफर दोहराई जाएगी। 
इसिलए यह समय reactive response का नहीं, बिÊक decisive और उिचत action का है।  
चेयरमैन सर, floods के root causes पर गंभीरता से ध्यान देना होगा। Dams मȂ बढ़ती siltation, 
illegal mining, encroachment और निदयȗ की बदलती river morphology पर काम करना 
होगा।  
चेयरमैन सर, भाखड़ा डैम
 की लगभग 9 billion cubic meters क्षमता मȂ से करीब 2 
billion cubic meters क्षमता siltation के कारण समाÃत हो चुकी है। इसी Ģकार गोिंबद सागर मȂ 
100 से 200 फीट तक गाद जमा होना एक alarming संकेत है िक हमारी water management 
system पर गंभीर खतरा मंडरा रहा है। 
चेयरमैन साहब, मȅ आगर्ह करता हूँ िक एक "Comprehensive Punjab Flood 
Resilience and Water Security Plan" 
तैयार िकया जाए, िजसमȂ flood-control dams और 
छोटे retention reservoirs का िनमार्ण िकया जाए, तथा मौजूदा बांधȗ को RCC lining या heavy 
sheet protection से मजबूत िकया जाए। Flood forecasting मȂ satellite mapping, AI-based 
rainfall modelling और digital flood alerts का Ëयापक उपयोग िकया जाए। हमȂ wetlands को  
एक natural flood buffer क
े  रूप मȂ revive करने की ज़रूरत है। इसके  अितिरƪ, एक  
Integrated River Health Index और Open Data Record बनाया जाए, तािक river water 
quality की quarterly public reporting हर तीन महीने मȂ जनता के सामने रखी जा सके।

Need for a comprehensive overhaul of the Civil Services Examination system 
SHRI P. WILSON (Tamil Nadu) : Mr. Chairman, Sir, the Union Public Service 
Commission stands as the gateway through wh ich lakhs of aspirants seek to realize 
their dreams, trusting it to be an engine of  equality of opportunity in matters of public 
employment and of participation in good gov ernance by joining public service. Yet, 
the Preliminary Examinations are conducted only in Hindi and English, while the Mains 
Examination permits all 22 scheduled language s. Limiting the Exams to only English 
and Hindi creates a barrier, disadvantaging  intelligent students from non-Hindi and 
non-English backgrounds, especially South Indian students from Tamil Nadu, Andhra 
Pradesh, Karnataka and Kerala.  
Sir, what kind of system is this that allows equality at the final stage, but denies 
it at the entry level itself? A student fr om Tamil Nadu, Karnataka, Kerala, Andhra 
Pradesh, West Bengal, Maharashtra or  the North East who has studied and 
developed intellectual abilities in his mother tongue is forced to compete in a language 
that does not reflect his true potential. Thousands are eliminated at the very first 
stage, not because they lack merit, but b ecause the system denies  linguistic justice. 
Many students from rural areas hesitate to  appear in the Civil Services because the 
Preliminary question paper is in English or  Hindi. The restriction creates an uneven 
10
[ RAJYA SABHA ]
10
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

level playing field and the denial of equal access to public employment violates the 
very basic right given in the Constitution of India. This is not me ritocracy; this is 
exclusion by design.  
Mr. Chairman, Sir, I demand that th e Preliminary Examinations must be 
conducted in all 22 scheduled languages immediately, including Tamil. Secondly, the 
examination cycle itself is excessively lo ng. It takes nearly 15 months from the 
Preliminary stage to the final selection. For an aspirant, one failed attempt means 
losing a year. With three attempts, four to five years of their prime youth are gone.  
Even the Parliamentary Standing Committee ha s flagged this excessive duration. Yet, 
the Government has offered no response. I urge the Government to shorten the cycle 
and consider conducting the Preliminary examination twice a year.   
Sir, third is the interview process. The personality test is opaque, unrecorded 
and unreviewable. There is no transparency, no accountability and no mechanism for 
scrutiny. There is a clear perception, and it  is increasingly evident, that the scoring 
pattern favours English fluenc y, urban exposure and elite institutional backgrounds.  
Further, there is a serious apprehension am ong the OBC, SC and ST aspirants that 
even when they secure marks on par with General Catego ry candidates in the Written 
examination, they are awarded disproport ionately lower marks in the Interview, 
resulting in lower ranks and reduced opportunities, and they are not found suitable.  
This is the systematic way in which they are eliminating OBC, SC and ST students. 
Sir, this strikes at the very heart of social justice.   
MR. CHAIRMAN: I think you are unjustified in your speech. 
SHRI P. WILSON : I think, the Government must di sclose the Interview marks on a 
Category and Board-wise basis and introduce a transparent system.

Need for a National Mission to protect and digitise ancient stone and copper plate 
inscriptions 
SHRIMATI SUDHA MURTY (Nominated) : Sir, Ashoka, Devanampiya Piyadasi written 
in Pali or Devanampriya Pr iyadarshi, was not known to  many people. He made a 
fantastic work for poor people of this co untry and he was the greatest Buddhist that 
India has ever seen. He is th e shining star in Indian history. But, nobody knew what 
his real name was. In 1915, a British offi cer by the name of Cecil Beadon came to a 
sleepy village known as Maski in  Raichur district in Karnataka. There, he saw a stone 
inscription on a hill and he said, “Here is Ashoka who is known as Devanampiyadasi 
Ashoka.” From that day onwards to this day,  we know the greatness of Ashoka. His 
name is everywhere in the world. The second  example I want to give you is about Gir 
Hill. If you go to Girnar Hill, on the right side, there are three insc riptions. One of them 
is by Ashoka, which explains how a king should behave and how people should be 
kept happy. Second is from the Gupta Dynasty, describing how they conquered 
India. The third is of Raja Rudradaman, who repaired a lake at his own expense 
without imposing any taxes.  Actually, all the stone in scriptions are not just 
inscriptions; they are the chain between the past and the present. If there are no 
stone inscriptions, we will never ever know  what it was 1,000 years back or 1,500 
years back. Actually, when you look at the statistics, 80 per cent of stone inscriptions 
date back 1,000 years. The stone inscriptions shared major information and later 
materials like paper, bhoja patra , tada patra , etc., came into use. So, stone 
inscriptions play an important role in our co untry's culture and history. Sir, in my work 
I have seen, -- this is my passion -- the stone inscriptions have become a stepping 
stone in some villages or a washing st one in some riverbeds. Once they are 
destroyed, we cannot get them back. I want  to congratulate our Government, which, 
in 2025 and 2026, instituted a new mission  known as Gyan Bharatam that actually 
collects all the manuscripts all over India. Rs. 491 crore has been assigned for this 
work for the period of five years, so that  we can save our manuscripts. If we do not 
know history, our past, we will not know ou r future. If one wants to be confident in 
our country, one should know the roots of our country, that is, our history. So, I 
request the Government of India to instit ute another mission, where we can save all 
our stone inscriptions, whether on gold, on copper plate or on stone, using the latest 
method of digitization.

matter raised by the hon’ble Member, Shrimati Sudha Murty:

Demand for mandatory QR codes on medicines and regulation against forced sale    
of full strips 
SHRI GOLLA BABURAO (Andhra Pradesh) : Sir, this House has expressed concern 
many times over increasing out-of-pocket expenses year-after-year on health.   
According to a study published in the Journal of the American Medical Association, 60 
per cent of medical expenditure by outpatie nts is on medicines and 30 per cent of 
medical expenditure by inpatients is on me dicines. And, one of the reasons behind 
this is that chemists are forcing patients to buy the entire strip of tablets or capsules 
when they need only 2 or 3 tablets, or 4 or  5 tables, when prescription is only for a 
day or two or three. There were protests and dharnas on this issue. So, by 
acknowledging problems that patients face, when chemists insist on selling entire 
strip of tablets or capsules, the Government  of India held consultations with pharma 
and medical devices industry to find solution to protect the interests of patients. This 
exercise was done three years ago. During  the discussion, even the Drugs Controller 
[ 30 March, 2026 ]
15

General of India was present. But, so far,  no positive results have come. So, to 
address this problem, there are a few areas which need to be explored.  The first one 
is to find new packing technology fo r medicines. The Government can adopt 
perforation technology in medicine strips which create precise, weak tear lines 
between individual tablets or capsule unit s. This helps easy separation. But, here, 
there is a problem. The problem is, individual tablet or capsule does not contain batch 
number, manufacturing, expiry date and details of salts. So, for this purpose, the 
Government can direct the pharma and medi cal devices industry to print QR Code or 
Bar Code on all medicines, so that if the patient purchases even a single tablet or a 
blister pack, he can get the details thro ugh QR Code. This way, each individual 
segment can have details. This will in crease affordability, reduce wastage of 
medicines and enables patients to buy only as  per the prescribed course.  As of now, 
there is no nationwide uniform law forbidding the compulsory sale of full strips as well 
as for putting mandatory QR Code on each ta blet or capsule. Hence, I appeal to the 
Government of India to either formulate law or amend the Drug Rules, 1945, for this 
purpose which will go a long way in re ducing out-of-pocket-expenses thereby 
lessening financial burden on patients.

Nadu) and Dr. Fauzia Khan (Maharashtra).

 Shriman Sukhendu Sekhar Ray. 
Need for safeguarding freedom of expression in Higher Educational Institutions 
SHRI SUKHENDU SEKHAR RAY (West Bengal) : Hon’ble Chairman, Sir, Article 
19(1)(a) of the Indian Constitution guar antees the Right to Freedom of Speech and 
Expression to all citizens, including studen ts, faculty and researchers in Universities. 
Higher Educational Institutions in our country  have historically been spaces for vibrant 
intellectual debate, where diverse political , social and cultural ideas are openly 
discussed. This environment plays a crucial role in nurturing critical thinking and 
active citizenship among young people. However, in recent years, the exercise of free 
expression on campuses has faced several challenges. In the 2025 update, in the 
Academic Freedom Index, India ranked at 156 out of 179 countries assessed in the 
Report. Instances of campus protests, re strictions on student gatherings and 
disciplinary actions against dissenting voic es have raised con cerns about shrinking 
spaces for dialogue. Universities encour age debates, seminars and student-led 
discussions without fear of censorship or  retaliation. Strengthening institutional 
autonomy and reducing undue political in terference are also essential steps in 
maintaining academic independence. Ultimately , preserving freedom of expression in 
Indian Universities requires a collectiv e effort from administrators a nd the 
Government.  
Sir, the Fundamental Duties of the citize ns mandated under Article 51A of the 
Constitution stipulates, “ It shall be the duty of every citizen to develop scientific 
temper, humanism and the spirit of inquiry and reform. ” This balance is vital for 
[ 30 March, 2026 ]
23

preparing informed responsible citizens wh o can contribute meaningfully to India's 
diverse and evolving society.  Sir, through you, I urge upon the Government to ensure 
protection of Fundamental Rights to Speech and Expression in Higher Educational 
Institutions so that students, academicians and researchers can have their discourses 
freely and without any fear and also to perform their Fundamental Duties, as 
enshrined in the Constitution. Thank you.

Demand for providing world-class facilities in 1000 ITIs across the country to 
accelerate the skill development through PM Sethu Scheme 
SHRI RYAGA KRISHNAIAH (Andhra Pradesh) : Respected, Chairman, Sir, I wish to 
raise a matter of urgent public import ance regarding the recently announced PM 
Sethu Scheme, which aims to give a major push to skill development by establishing 
world-class facilities in 1,000 Industrial Training Institutes (ITIs) across the country.  
This flagship initiative is expected to  transform India's skilling ecosystem by 
modernizing ITI infrastructure, bringing in advanced technology, strengthening 
industry partnerships, and equipping youth wi th globally competitive skills.  However, 
there is lack of clarity on several criti cal aspects of the scheme, especially with 
respect to the following:  The criteria used to  select 1,000 ITIs for transformation;  the 
timeline and phases of implementation across States;  the extent of financial support 
to be provided by the Centre and modalitie s for State participation; the nature of 
curriculum upgrades, smart classrooms, mo dern labs, digital tools, and industry-
linked courses planned; the mechanism for training and certification of faculty to meet 
global standards; and the proposed monito ring framework to ensure transparency, 
quality and accountability,  also, the role of  private sector, sect or skill councils, and 
international partners in implementing the world-class skilling ecosystem given that 
millions of young people depend on ITIs for technical education and employment, and 
considering the transformative potential of the PM Sethu Scheme. Sir, I want the 
24

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Government to kindly clarify about the comprehensive roadmap, funding architecture, 
institutional guidelines, and quality-assu rance mechanisms proposed under the PM 
Sethu Scheme for establishing world-class skill development facilities in 1,000 ITIs 
and how the Government plans to ensure timely execution, industry relevance and 
high employability outcomes for the youth of the country.

*376. [The questioner was absent.] 
Inspection of fuel control switch in flights 
*376.    SHRI C. VE. SHANMUGAM:  Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased 
to state:-                
 (a) whether it is a fact that Governme nt has directed all airline operators to 
complete inspection of the locking mechanism of the fuel control switch on 
their fleet; 
(b)   if so, the details thereof; 
(c)   whether the airline operators have complied with these directives; and 
(d)   if so, the details thereof and if not, the reasons therefor? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION (SHR I KINJARAPURAMMOHAN NAIDU): (a) to 
(d) A statement is laid on the table of the house. 
Statement 
 (a) to (d) In July 2025, Directorate Gene ral of Civil Aviation (DGCA) issued an 
order mandating all applicable operators to inspect the locking mechanism of the fuel 
control switch on the Boeing fleet of aircraft in the country as per Special 
Airworthiness Information Bulletin (SAIB)  NM-18-33 issued by Federal Aviation 
Administration (FAA). Airlines operators ha ve carried out the required inspections 
and all aircraft found satisfactory for operation.

weather. It depends on multiple other th ings because there might be other flights

there, and what services should be provided  to the passenger within one hour; it is

MR. CHAIRMAN:   Now, Q. No. 377.   
Monitoring of drinking water quality in rural and urban areas 
#*377. SHRI PRADIP KUMAR VARMA: Will the Minister of JAL SHAKTI be pleased to 
state: 
 (a) the number of water sources under gone laboratory-based quality testing 
during the last three years; 
(b)  the number of water sources affected  by Arsenic, Fluoride, Iron and other 
contaminants; 
(c)  the number of water quality testing laboratories operational to ensure safe 
drinking water; and 
(d) the number of remediation projects proposed by Government for areas 
affected by contaminated drinking water? 
THE MINISTER OF JAL SHAKTI (SHRI C R PATIL): (a) to (d) A Statement of reply is 
laid on the table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) to (d) Jal Jeevan Mission (JJM) is under implementa tion since August, 
2019, in partnership with States/ UTs to make provision of potable tap water supply in 
adequate quantity, of prescribed quality and on regular & long-term basis to rural 
households. Under JJM, as per existing gu idelines, Bureau of Indian Standards’ 
BIS:10500 standards are adopted as benchm ark for quality of water being supplied 
through the piped water supply schemes. Drinking Water being a State subject, the 
responsibility of Planning, Designing, Approval, Implementation, Operation & 
Maintenance of drinking water supply schemes, including those under the Jal Jeevan 
Mission (JJM), is vested with State/UT  Governments. The Government of India 
                                                           
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
36
[ RAJYA SABHA ]
36
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

supplements the efforts of the States/ UTs by providing financial, policy guidance and 
technical assistance. 
 Under JJM, while planning water supply schemes to provide tap water supply 
to households, priority is given to habitations affected by chemical contaminants.  
States/ UTs have been advised to plan and implement piped water supply schemes 
based on alternative safe water sources  including surface wa ter sources for the 
villages with water quality issues. 
 Under JJM, to enable the States/UTs for online reporting of water quality 
monitoring and surveillance including test re ports of water sample s for water quality, 
sample collection etc. for drinking water, an online JJM – Water Quality Management 
Information System (WQMIS) portal has been developed.Further, in consultation with 
various stakeholders 'Concise Handbook for Monitoring Water Quality of Piped 
Drinking Water Supply to Rural Households’ has been released in December 2024 for 
guidance to States/UT’s. This handbook recommended for a comprehensive testing 
of drinking water samples at various testin g points such as sour ce, treatment plant, 
storage and distribution points, and remedi al action wherever necessary, to ensure 
that the water supplied to households is of prescribed quality (BIS:10500). 
 As reported by States/UTs on JJM -WQMIS, the number of water sourcesand 
total no. of samples from such sources test ed in laboratory in last three yearsare 
given below:

 As reported by States/ UTs on JJM-WQMIS, as on 24.03.2026, the State 
Public Health Engineering/ Rural Water Supply Departments operate a network of 
2,874 water quality testing laboratories at va rious levels viz. State, regional, district, 
sub-division, block, mobile and/ or WTP f acility laboratories. Out of these, there are 
1,710 laboratories are accredited/ recognised.  
 For groundwater quality assessment, Central Ground Water Board (CGWB) 
generates groundwater quality data at a regional scale across the country, through its 
groundwater quality monitoring programme and scientific studies as per the approved 
Standard Operating Procedure (SOP). Data on ground water quality available with 
CGWB are made available in public domain  through reports as well as through the 
web site (http://www.cgwb.gov.in) for use by various stakeholders. The data is also 
shared with concerned State Governments for taking necessary remedial measures. 
Further, to ensure safe drinking water an d mitigate groundwater contamination by 
various pollutants, a combination of preventive, treatment, and management 
strategies has been suggested in the Annual Ground Water Quality Report – 2025. 
 The number of groundwater samples anal ysed by CGWB during the last three 
available monitoring years are given below:  
S. No. Year Total No. of groundwater 
samples analysed by CGWB  
1 2022 15,407 
2 2023 15,259 
3 2024 14,978

[ RAJYA SABHA ]
38
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 CGWB has also informed that under the National Aquifer Mapping Programme 
(NAQUIM) of CGWB, special attention is be ing given to the aspect of ground water 
quality including contamination by toxic su bstances in ground water on issues like 
salinity ingress, alkalinity etc. which are be ing taken up on priority basis for NAQUIM 
studies. 
 For Arsenic contamination, CGWB has developed the innovative cement 
sealing technique for construction of Arseni c-free wells in the Gangetic flood plains. 
CGWB is providing technical assistance to  State agencies to enable tapping of 
contamination-free aquifers and facilitatin g construction of arsenic-safe wells.To 
address Fluoride contamination, Central Ground Water Board (CGWB) has provided 
technical assistance and proposed an eco -friendly, sustainable natural recharge 
model. This model involves constructing wells near to the surface water bodies that 
tap into the shallow water table aquifer wi thin colluvium, alluvium, and the weathered 
sections of granitic/basaltic areas, at depths ranging from 18 to 30 meters. 
 The Ministry of Housing and Urban A ffairs (MoHUA) has informed that Urban 
Local Bodies (ULBs)/ Parastatal conduct te sting of water at Water Treatment Plants 
(WTPs) and household levels as per applicab le norms.  As reported by MoHUA, the 
compliance status for E. coli, Arsenic, Fl uoride testing of Water Quality Samples 
under urban locations in AMRUT 2.0 during  the period 01.01.2024 – 31.12.2024 are 
as under: 
Sampling Location Total Samples Tested Compliant Samples 
Water Treatment Plants (WTPs) 3,35,278 3,32,170 
Household Level (Urban Areas) 22,45,200 22,18,838 
 So far, 6140 Million Litre per Day (M LD) of WTP capacity has been approved 
under AMRUT, of which, 5,429 MLD WTP ca pacity has been created. Under AMRUT 
2.0, 11,395 MLD WTP capacity ha s been approved so far. These treatment plants are 
generally equipped with inhous e lab facilities or have tie up with the Labs for regular 
water quality testing. 
 MoHUA has published Manual on Water Supply and Treatment Systems (Drink 
from Tap) in March 2024 for referenc e by the States/ULBs for designing, 
implementation, drinking water quality and monitoring of the water supply projects. 
[ 30 March, 2026 ]
39

(https://mohua.gov.in/publication/manual-on-water-supplyandtreatment-systems-
--drink-from-tap---march-2024.php). 
 Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB)  is also monitoring ground water 
quality under National Water Quality Mo nitoring Programme (NWMP) at 1271 
locations in the country. Industry Specific Discharge Standards and General 
Standards notified by the Central Government under the Environment (Protection) 
Rules,1986 are enforced by the State Pollution Control Boards (SPCBs) and Pollution 
Control Committees (PCCs) through consen t mechanism in respective State/UT. 
Based on the water quality data of Gr ound Water locations, CPCB requested 
concerned SPCBs/PCCs to identify sources of pollution at non-complying locations 
and take necessary corrective actions, actions such as:– 
i. Sealing of hand pumps/ wells having contaminated ground water 
ii. Display sign board indicating (Not Fit for Drinking Water purposes) in 
vernacular language. 
iii. Arrangement of alternate drinking water supply in the affected areas 
The States/UTs are taking testing and remediation measures towards ensuring 
safe drinking water supply in due consideration of above facts.

MR. CHAIRMAN: Now, Q. No. 378.  
Implementaion of MSE Cluster Development Programme 
*378. SHRI SANA SATHISH BABU: Will th e Minister of MICRO, SMALL AND 
MEDIUM ENTERPRISES be pleased to state: 
(a) the number of clusters identified under the Micro and Small Enterprises 
Cluster Development Programme across the country, particularly in the 
State of Andhra Pradesh,along with location of cluster and sector details; 
(b)  the number of Common Facility Centres established under the programme in 
the State, district-wise; 
(c)  the details of projects approved under the scheme, includ ing new Industrial 
Infrastructure Development Projects an d upgradation of existing industrial 
estates in the State, district-wise; and 
(d)  the district-wise funds allocated, released and utilised for these projects in 
the State, along with their present status and employment generated? 
THE MINISTER FOR MICRO, SMALL AN D MEDIUM ENTERPRISES (SHRI JITAN 
RAM MANJHI): (a) to (d) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
44
[ RAJYA SABHA ]
44
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Statement 
 (a) to (d) The Micro and Small Enterp rises - Cluster Development Programme 
(MSE-CDP) is a Central Sector Scheme aimed at enhancing productivity and 
competitiveness of MSMEs through cluste r-based interventions. Under the scheme, 
Common Facility Centre (CFC) proposals are identified by MSME stakeholders and 
forwarded by State Governments after due examination, while Infrastructure 
Development (ID) projects are identified by State Governments and recommended 
for approval. Government grants are releas ed in phases, based on the progress of 
the projects. 
In all 606 projects have been approv ed in the country under MSE-CDP, of 
which 47 projects are in Andhra Pradesh.  These include 11 Common Facility Centres 
and 36 Infrastructure Development projects. Out of 47 projects approved in Andhra 
Pradesh, 5 CFCs and 12 Infrastructure Development projects stand completed and 
are operational. As per information received from State Government, these projects 
have generated employment of approximately 8300 Nos. in CFC Projects and 57793 
Nos. in ID Projects. 
The details of common facility centers, new Industrial Infrastructure 
Development Projects and upgradation of ex isting industrial estates in Andhra 
Pradesh, district-wise approved projects along with cluster location and sector are 
enclosed as Annexure.

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, Q.No.379. 
Irrigat
ion project development under PMKSY in Telangana 
*379. Dr. K. LAXMAN: Will the Minister of JAL SHAKTI be pleased to state: 
 (a) the details of central assistance provided under the Pradhan Mantri Krishi 
Sinchayee Yojana (PMKSY) for the completion of ongoing major and 
[ 30 March, 2026 ]
55

medium irrigation projects and micro-ir rigation in the State of Telangana 
during the last three years; 
(b) the technical and financial suppo rt extended by the Central Water 
Commission (CWC) for the review and optimisation of large projects like the 
Kaleshwaram Lift Irrigation Project; and 
(c)  the expected impact of these irrigati on projects on agricultural productivity, 
farmer incomes and drought-proofing in the State of Telangana? 
THE MINISTER OF JAL SHAKTI  (SHRI C R PATIL): (a) to (c) A statement is laid on 
the Table of the House.  
Statement 
 (a) Ninty-Nine (99) ongoing Major/Medium Irrigation projects (and 7 
phases) included under Pradhan Mantri Krishi Sinchayee Yojana (PMKSY) during the 
year 2016-17, with the aim to enhance phys ical access of water on farm and expand 
cultivable area under assured irrigation , improve on-farm water use efficiency, 
introduce sustainable water conservation pr actices etc. Out of them, eleven (11) 
projects of Telangana were included un der PMKSY-AIBP with  eligible central 
assistance of Rs. 1129.80 Crore.  A total central assistance of Rs. 981.49 Crore has 
been provided for these projects.  No eligible proposal for Central assistance received 
from Govt. of Telangana during the last three years. 
 Per Drop More Crop (PDMC) scheme, being implemented by Department of 
Agriculture and Farmer’s Welfare, was impl emented under the umbrella of PMKSY till 
March, 2022 and now being implemented und er Pradhan Mantri - Rashtriya Krishi 
Vikash Yojna (PM-RKVY). PDMC scheme focuses on enhancing water use efficiency 
at the farm level through micro-irrigation techniques, namely drip and sprinkler 
systems. Total central assistance of Rs. 873.95 Crore has been provided to 
Telangana under PDMC, out of which, Rs. 194.63 Crore provided during the last three 
years. 
 (b)  Central Water Commission (CWC) under Department of Water 
Resources, River Development and Gang a Rejuvenation (DoWR, RD & GR) 
provides techno-economic appraisal of majo r and medium irrigation projects. This 
includes assessment of water availability, examination of inter-State aspects, 
irrigation planning, overall techno-economi c viability of the project etc. It also 
monitors projects included under ongoing schemes of DoWR, RD & GR for central 
assistance. 
56
[ RAJYA SABHA ]
56
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 In addition, CWC extends technical su pport in formulation of projects and 
design-related aspects at the request of the state government. 
 (c) Irrigation potential of 424.59 thousa nd hectare has been created under 
eleven (11) PMKSY-AIBP projects in Tela ngana against their ultimate irrigation 
potential of 585.10 thousand hectare. Furth er, micro irrigation has been developed in 
385 thousand hectare area of Telangana under PDMC scheme since 2015-16. 
 As informed by the Government of Telangana, within the command area of 
completed large irrigation projects of the Stat e, agricultural productivity increased by 
about 2.3 times.

*380. [The questioner was absent.] 
Apprenticeship programmes in airport ground handling and air traffic services 
*380. SHRIMATI SULATA DEO: Will the Minister of CIVIL AVIATION be pleased to 
state:-                   
(a)  whether apprenticeship programmes in airport ground handling and air traffic 
services are being expanded in the State of Odisha; and 
(b)   if so, the details thereof? 
THE MINISTER OF CIVIL AVIATION (S HRI KINJARAPURAMMOHAN NAIDU): (a) 
and (b) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
 (a) and (b) Apprenticeship and skill deve lopment in airport ground handling 
and related operational areas are undertake n by Ground Handling Agencies (GHAs) 
as part of their operational requiremen ts. GHAs recruit personnel and impart 
necessary training, including structured cla ssroom training, on-the-job training and 
periodic refresher programmes. Further, skilling initiatives encompass a wide range of 
areas such as passenger handling, baggage  management, ramp operations, safety 
procedures, emergency response, customer  service and operation of specialised 
equipment. These programmes are aligned with broader Government policies on skill 
development and are implemented from time  to time in accordance with sectoral 
requirements. 
 The training and capacity building activit ies are also undertaken in collaboration 
with airlines, training institutes and othe r service providers associated with airport 
operations. Such apprenticeship and trai ning activities are conducted across the 
country, including in the State of Odisha, based on operational requirements.

ﻮﺕѧѧیﮨ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺎﻝѧѧѧѧیﺱیﭘ ﺎﻧﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﺑﻨ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ہѧѧѧ۔ںیﻧ ﮨﺎںی ﻮﺉѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺮیﮐѧѧѧѧﺋ ﭧѧѧﻟﻤ ﺍی ﻑیﺱ ﺎﺭڈѧѧѧѧﮔ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ 
ﻞѧѧ ѧﺍﺻ ںیﻡ ﺍﺱ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺰیﺍѧѧѧѧﻮیکیﮔѧѧѧѧﻭﭨ ﺧﻮﺩ یﻩ ﺎﻧﻮﻥѧѧѧѧѧﻗ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﻨﺎﻧ ﺮﻭﻉѧѧѧﺷ ﮑﺘﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺳ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺍﺱ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ 
ﺎﺭﻝѧѧѧѧﭩﺮیﺍﻡیﭘѧѧѧѧѧѧیﻧ ﭧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭧیﺍﮐﺎﺅﻧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﺑﻠ ﺰﻭﺭѧѧѧﮐﻤ ﻮѧѧﮨ یگیﺟﺎﺉ ﺍﻭﺭ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﺭیﺁﺭﺑ ﻠﻮںیﻑѧѧѧѧѧﺻ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺘﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﺭﺍﺳ ﻮﻻѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻬ 
ﺟﺎ ﮑﺘﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ
ѧﺳ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ 
ﺮیﺕѧѧѧیﺳ ﺎﺕѧѧѧѧ،ﺑ ﺍٓﺋﯽ یپ ﺱیﺍ ﺮﺱیﺁﻑѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ﺮیﻥیﺱѧѧѧѧﺋ ﻮﺯѧѧѧѧﻨﺲیﭘѧѧѧѧѧﺷ ںیﻡ ﺟﻮ ﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻓﮑ 
ہѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻮﭨ ﻁﮯ ﺍیک ﺟﺎ ﺭﮨﺎ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺍﺱ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ڈﺭیک ﺮﺱیﺁﻑѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺗﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﺎﻑѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﻧﺎﺍﻧﺼ ﻮﺕѧѧیﮨ 
ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺎﮨﮯѧѧﭼ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ ہѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺗﺠﺮﺑ ﺍی ﺭٹیﻡ ﻬѧѧѧѧ ѧﮐﭽ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﻮѧѧ،ﮨ ﺍٓﺋﯽ یپ ﺱیﺍ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺍٓﺋﯽ ﺍے ﺱیﺍ ﺮﺱѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺁﻓﺴ یک ﻢѧѧѧѧیﮐ 
ﮯѧѧ
ѧﺳ ﺟﻮ ﻫﺎﺟﻮﺝ ﺟﺎ ﺭﮨﺎ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺐѧѧѧѧﺗ ﻩی ﺍﻭﺭ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺍﺩﻩیﺯ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮏیﻡیﭘﺮﺍﺑѧѧѧﭨ ﻮѧѧﮨ ﺎیﺟﺎﺉѧѧѧѧ۔ﮔ 
ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺍﺱ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ںیﻡ ﺍﺩﻩیﺯ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺗ ﺮﻭﺭѧѧ ѧیﺿ ﻤﺲѧѧﺍﺅںیﺳ ﺮѧѧѧѧﭘ ﻮﺉѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﻮﮐﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻓ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ یﺍﺱ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﺎﺗﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﻩی ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ڈﺭیک ﺮﺱیﺁﻑѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻼﻑѧѧﺧ ﺮﺍﻥѧѧѧѧیﭘ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﮕﺎﺗ ﺎﻑѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﻧﺎﺍﻧﺼ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﮯѧѧѧѧﺁﮔ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ
ѧﺑﮍﻫﺎﺗ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی ﺮﺱیﺁﻑѧѧѧﺳ ﺎﻟﻮںѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﮏѧѧѧﺗ ﺨﺖѧѧѧﺳ ﮉیﻑѧѧѧﻟ ﮉѧѧѧѧѧѧﻨﺲیﮐﻨѧѧѧѧѧﺷ ںیﻡ ﺧﺪﻣﺖ ﮯیﺩѧѧѧѧﺗ ںیﻩ ﺍﻭﺭ 
ﺜﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﮐ کیﺍ یﻩ ﻦѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﺮﻣﻮﺷ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ 15 ﮯѧѧѧﺳ 18 ﺎﻝѧѧѧﺳ ﮏѧѧѧﺗ ﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﺘﻈ ﺮﺗﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ ۔ںیﻩ ﺩﺱ ﺎﻝѧѧѧﺳ ﺐѧѧیﻟﻤ 
ﺎﻧﻮﻥѧѧѧѧѧیﻗ ﮍﺍﺉѧѧѧیﻟ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺪѧѧѧѧѧ،ﺑﻌ ﺟﺐ ﻮںѧѧѧﺍﻧﮩ ﮯѧѧѧﻧ ﻦѧѧѧѧیﺍﭘ ﺎﻥѧѧѧѧѧﭘﮩﭽ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺭیﮐ ﺮﻭﮔﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻦیﭘѧѧѧﺷ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ﺣﻖ 
ﻞѧѧѧﺣﺎﺻ ،ﺍیک ﺐѧѧѧѧﺗ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﮑ ےیﻝ ﺮیﻥیﺱѧѧѧѧﺋ ﻮﺯѧѧѧѧﻦیﭘѧѧ ѧﺷ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺘہѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺭﺍﺳ ﺮѧѧѧѧѧﭘﻬ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﺪѧѧѧѧѧѧ ѧﺑﻨ ﺍیک ﺟﺎ ﺎѧﺭﮨ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی 
ﻞѧѧ ѧﺍﺻ ںیﻡ ﺍﺱ ﺎﺕѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺩﮐﻬﺎﺗ ےﻩ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﭩﺮیﺱѧѧѧѧѧѧﻧ ﭩﺮیﺍѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻮیڈﻣﻨﺴѧѧѧѧﭨ ﭩﺮﻭﻝѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻨ یک ﭧیﺍﻑѧѧѧѧﺱیﻭﻥیﮐ ﺮѧѧѧѧﭘ 
ﺯﻭﺭ ﺩے ﺭﮨﺎ ﮯѧѧ،ﮨ ﺟﺲ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺩﻫﺎیﺱ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﺛ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻤﺒ ﺩﻭﺭ ںیﻡ ﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺍﻧﭩﺮﻧ ﻮﺭٹیﺱѧѧѧѧیﮐ ﺮѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﭘﮍےﮔ 
ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﻩی ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ﻞѧѧ ѧﺍﺻ ﻮںѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺩﻗﺘ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺱیڈﺭیﺍ یﻩ ہѧѧѧںیﻧ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ۔ﮐﺮﺗ یﺱ ﺍے یپ ﻑیﺍ ںیﻡ ﮓѧѧﻟ ﮓѧѧѧ ѧﺑﻬ 
93,000 ﺲیکیﻭѧѧѧﺯیﻧ ۔ںیﻩ ﺮﻭﭨﻢѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭧیﺭﮐѧѧѧﻧ ںیﻡ ﮯیڈѧѧѧ،ﻟ ﺮѧѧѧѧﮓیﭨѧѧѧѧﻧﻨ ںیﻡ ،پیگ ﻨﻞѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﺮﺳ ﮓیﻑѧѧѧѧѧﭨﻨ - 
ﻩی ﺐѧѧѧﺳ ﻞѧѧ ѧیﺍﺻ ﺰѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﺍﺋﺴ ۔ںیﻩ ﻦیﻝѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮﮐﺎﺭѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮﮐﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻓ ﺍﺱ ﺎﺕѧѧѧѧﺑ ﺮѧѧѧѧﭘ ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮѧѧѧѧﺍﻭﭘ ﻮﻥѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯیﺏѧѧѧѧѧﭨﻬ 
ﺎѧѧѧѧ،ﮔ ہѧѧѧﻧ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮉѧѧѧѧѧѧﮔﺮﺍﺅﻧ ﺮѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺍیک ﻮѧѧﮨ ﺭﮨﺎ ﮯѧѧﮨ !ﮑﮯ ѧѧѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﻼﻭﻩѧѧ،ﻋ ﺟﻮ ﺯیچ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﮩﻠ کیﺍ ﺰیﺍѧѧѧѧﻮیکیﮔѧѧѧѧﻭﭨ 
ﺮѧѧѧѧﺲیﭘѧѧѧѧѧѧѧ
ѧﮐﭩ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧ،یﺗ ﺍﺏ ﮯѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﺎﻧﻮﻥѧѧѧѧѧیﻗ ﺭﻭپ ﺍیﺩ ﺟﺎ ﺭﮨﺎ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﭘﮩﻠ ﻢѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻢѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﻝѧѧѧѧﺯیﺱیﭘ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺞیچѧѧѧѧѧﻟﻨ 
                                                           
 †Transliteration in Urdu script.

72

ﺍی ﻭیﺭﻭ ﺍیک ﺟﺎ ﮑﺘﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﺎѧѧѧѧ،ﺗﻬ ﺍﺏ ﮯѧѧѧﺍﺳ ﺎﺍﺕѧѧ ѧﻧ ﺟﮉیﺭ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﻨ ﺍیﺩ ﺍیگ ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﺁﮔ ﺪﻟﻨﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮑﻞѧѧѧѧѧﻣﺸ ﻮѧѧﮨ 
ﺎیﺟﺎﺉѧѧѧѧ۔ﮔ 
ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺍﺱ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺗﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﭩﺮیﺱѧѧѧѧѧѧﻧ یک ﺕیﻥ ﺎﻑѧѧﺻ ﻮѧѧﮨ یﺟﺎﺕ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی ﺎﺭﻡیﺭѧѧѧѧﻓ ہѧѧѧ،ںیﻧ ﭩﺮﻭﻝѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻨ 
یک ﺶѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻮﺷ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺭﺍیﻡ ﻮﺍﻝѧѧѧﺳ ﮯѧѧﮨ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮﻡѧѧﮨ ﭩﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﻣﻨﺴ ﭙﺮѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﻡیﺳ ﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧﮐ یک ﺎﺭٹѧѧѧѧ ѧیﺍﺗﻬ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯیکѧѧѧﺳ ﺮѧѧѧﻧﻈ 
ﺪﺍﺯѧѧ
ѧﺍﻧ ﺮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮑﺖѧѧѧѧѧیﺳ ﮯѧѧﮨ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺎﻧﻮﻥѧѧѧѧѧﻗ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺭﻩѧѧѧѧѧﺩﻭﺑ ﻬѧѧѧѧѧѧﻟﮑ ﺮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮﺭﺱѧѧѧѧѧﺯیﻓ یک ﭗیﻝѧѧѧڈﺭﺷ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺮیکѧѧѧѧ ѧﭘﭽ ﺮﻧﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ 
یک ﺶѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻮﺷ ﺮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮑﺖѧѧѧѧѧیﺳ ﮯ؟ѧѧﮨ ﺮیﻭڈیﺝѧѧѧیﺷ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺰﻭﺭѧѧѧﮐﻤ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﻧ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺮیﺱѧѧѧѧﻦیﭘѧѧѧﺷ ﺍٓﻑ ﺎﻭﺭﺱѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺍُﻟﻨﮕﻬﻦ 
ﻬѧѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺍﺱ ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ہѧѧѧﺣﺼ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ یﻥیﺍ ﭧیﺟﺠﻢѧѧѧ،ﻧ ﺮѧѧѧѧیڈﮐ ﺍﻭﺭ ﺁﺭڈﺭ ﺍٓﻑ یﻥیﻉ ﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺋﮉﻻﺋﻦѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺳ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﺮﻧ  
ﻝیﺵیﻭڈیﺝ ﻭیﺭﻭ ﺮѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺩﻫﺎیﺱ ہѧѧѧﺣﻤﻠ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ(... ﺖѧѧѧﻭﻗ یک ﭧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﮔﻬﻨ )۔۔۔  ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ کیﺍ ﭧѧѧ۔ﻣﻨ ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ یﺭیﻡ 
ﺎﺭٹѧѧѧѧیﭘ ﺎѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎѧѧѧѧﻢﭨѧѧѧѧﺋ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﺰیﺍѧѧѧѧﻭیﻭٹیکیﮔ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻩی ﺎﺭیﺍﺩﻫѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎیﺩѧѧ ѧﻧ ہѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻭﻩ ﺰѧѧѧﺭﻭﻟ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻋﮯیﺫﺭ ﻮﺭٹѧѧѧѧﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ 
ﻠﮯیﻑѧѧѧѧѧﺻ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎﺉѧѧѧѧیﺑ ﺎﺱѧѧѧѧﭘ ﺮےѧѧѧѧ،ﮐ ﺮیﻭڈیﺝѧѧѧیﺷ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻥیڈﻭﻡ ںیﻡ ﺩﺧﻞ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی ﻮںیﺕѧѧѧﻧ ﺲѧѧѧѧѧѧﺁﺭﮔﻨ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ 
چیﺏ ﺲیﻝیﺏѧѧѧﻧ ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺎѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﺑﮕﺎڑﺗ ﮯѧ ѧﮨ -  ﺮیﻭڈیﺝѧѧѧ،یﺷ ﻞیﻝѧѧѧ،ﭼﺮیﺟﺴ ﺰکیﺍѧѧѧѧ۔ﻭیﻭٹیﮔ ﮯیﺍѧѧѧﺳ ﺮﻭﻭﺯѧѧѧѧﺲیﭘѧѧѧﻧ 
ﻨﻮѧѧѧѧѧﮏیﺳѧѧﺩﻫﺎﻧ ﺭﻭپ ﮯѧѧѧﺳ ﻨﺪѧѧѧѧѧﻫیﺳ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ﻬѧѧѧѧﺭےیﮔ ںیﻡ ﺎﺗﮯѧѧѧѧﺁﺟ ،ںیﻩ ہیکѧѧѧѧѧﻭﻧﮑ ﻩی کیچ ﮉیﺍѧѧѧﻧ ﺲیﻝیﺏѧѧѧﻧ 
ﻮѧѧѧѧﮐ ﺰﻭﺭѧѧѧﮐﻤ ﺮﺗﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ ،ںیﻩ ﺟﻮ ﺮیڈѧѧѧیﺱیﻣﻮﮐ ﮯѧѧѧѧﮐ ےیﻝ ﺮﻭﺭѧѧ ѧیﺿ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ ﻩی ﻞѧѧѧѧﺑ کیﺍ ﮉѧѧѧﻞیﺍﻧѧѧѧѧﯽﮐѧѧѧѧﺭڈﻳ 
ﺮﺟﻨﺲیﺍѧѧѧیﻣ یﺱیﺝ ﺘﻬﺖѧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﺍﺳ ﺩﺍیپ ﺮﻧﮯѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐ یک ﺶѧѧѧѧѧѧﮐﻮﺷ ﮯѧѧ۔ﮨ(... ﺖѧѧѧﻭﻗ یک ﭧѧѧѧѧѧѧیﮔﻬﻨ )۔۔۔ ﺮѧѧѧ،ﺳ ﺮﻑѧѧ ѧﺻ

SHRI TIRUCHI SIVA (Tamil Nadu) : Mr. Deputy Chairman, Sir, again, I stand here to 
oppose a Bill, that has been introduced and brought for consideration, with much 
pain and agony. We all know very well that  even at the introduction stage, all the 
opposition parties opposed the introduction of the Bill itself. It negates the landmark 
judgment of the Supreme Court. It also raises a question of separation of powers.  
Sir, this Bill is about the CAPF, Central Armed Police Forces, which consists of 
Central Reserve Police Force, BSF, Indo Tibetan Border Police, Assam Riffles and 
CISF. Around 13,00,000 personnel are work ing in it and 15,00,000 have already 
retired. I would like to, very humbly, present before this House, that in the past three 
years, 438 personnel have died by committing suicide. In the past three years, 50,000 
have resigned their jobs from  the CAPF. What is the reason  for that? It is because of 
the stress that they have. There are instance s of work overload, slow promotion, little

suggestions to strengthen the Act.

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA): Shri Niranjan Bishi; six minutes.  
SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha) : Mr. Vice-Chairman, Sir, I thank you for giving me 
this opportunity to speak on the Ce ntral Armed Police Forces (General 
Administration) Bill, 2026, which aims to streamline administration and improve 
coordination among our Central Forces like CRPF, BSF, ITBP and CISF. But there are 
several serious concerns which must be addressed before its effective 
implementation.  
 Sir, in this Bill, there is jurisdictio
nal confusion with State Police. During joint 
operations, a lack of clarity in command st ructure may lead to operational problems.  
The Bill is silent on technology and moderniz ation. There are no binding provisions for 
modern equipment, cyber capability or su rveillance systems. In today’s security 
environment, I find this is a serious gap.  Human rights safeguards are not clearly 
defined in this Bill. In sensitive areas lik e LWE-affected region s and border zones, 
strong safeguards are necessary to maintain trust and India’s global image. 
 Sir, over-centralisation of power is a major issue. This Bill appears to give 
excessive control to the Government of In dia and the Ministry of Home Affairs. Our 
Forces operate in diverse conditions from borders to internal security, whereas 
limiting their autonomy may reduce their oper ational efficiency. In  this Bill, modern 
internal security challenges are seen. Maoist  insurgencies in Odisha’s forests, urban 
terror threats or complex border manageme nt, are inherently multi-dimensional and 
interlinked. At the heart of this coordination framework lies the leadership of the Indian 
Police Service, which acts as the indispen sable integrating link in this entire 
ecosystem.  
 Sir, for ensuring successful prosecuti on, investigation is the most critical 
stage, and all investigating officers – whether from Central agencies or State  
Police — function under the professional supervision and guidan ce of IPS Officers. 
Therefore, IPS officers do not merely co ordinate operations but they ensure the 
[ 30 March, 2026 ]
111

continuity between intelligence, action, invest igation and conviction. If we weaken or 
reduce the role of IPS officers in CAPFs, we risk breaking this vital chain. Sir, this 
year, many districts of Odisha, including Bolangir, became naxal-free. Fifteen cadres 
of BBN division surrendered. It is a credit that goes completely to the Odisha Police.   
 The Bill lacks a strong and independen t grievance redressal mechanism. Our 
jawans and officers face issues related to  promotions, service conditions and even 
harassment. If complaints are handled inte rnally without independence, justice may 
not be ensured. There is ambiguity in se rvice conditions. Differences in pay scales, 
promotions and retirement age still exist across forces. Without proper 
harmonization, this will continue to creat e dissatisfaction among personnel. The Bill 
does not adequately address mental health  and welfare. CAPF personnel work under 
extreme stress, long deployments and separa tion from families. There is no clear 
provision for mandatory counselling, prop er leave policy or stress management 
systems. While the Bill talks about coordination , it risks diluting the unique identity of 
each force. A one-size-fits-all approach ma y not work for forces handling different 
responsibilities like border security and inte rnal law and order situation. There is lack 
of parliamentary oversight. The Bill do es not mandate regular reporting or 
independent audits. When such large forces  and budgets are involved, transparency 
is essential. I would also like to highlight a very serious issue of vacancies in CAPFs.  
As per recent data, more than  93,000 posts are laying vacan t. This is affecting both 
border and internal security, despite increas ing budget allocations under the Ministry 
of Home Affairs. There are also concerns regarding SC/ST representation. While 
reservation policies exist, backlog vacancies  are not filled up and representation in 
higher ranks remains limited as CCR/APAR of SC/ST made adverse. 
While this Bill is a step towards re form, its success depends on balancing 
Central control with operational balancing. &“Shri Biju Patnaik is the blessed son of 
India, Indonesia, and Russia. He is an ac claimed leader, former Chief Minister of 
Odisha, architect of modern India and a freedom fighter. He deserves the highest 
regard. A grave disrespect and insult has b een directed towards him, and for that, I 
demand strong action.  This incident is utterly deplorable.”

THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA) : Shri Ravi Chandra Vaddiraju; seven 
minutes.  
SHRI RAVI CHANDRA VADDIRAJU (Telangana): Sir, I rise today to speak on the 
important subject of Central Armed Police Fo rces (General Administration) Bill, 2026. 
The Central Armed Police Forces  or CAPFs are the backbone of our internal security 
system. Forces like CRPF, BSF, CISF, ITBP , and SSB work day and night to protect 
our borders, maintain law & order and en sure national security. Whether it is 
guarding our borders, fighting terrorism, managing international conflicts or protecting 
critical infrastructures, our CAPF personnel  serve the nation with dedication, 
discipline and courage. However, while their role is critical,  their administrative 
systems have often been complex,  fragmented and outdated.   Different forces follow 
different rules and procedures. This creates confusion, delays and inefficiencies. This 
is where the Central Armed Police Forces  (General Administration) Bill, 2026 
becomes important. सर, मȅ इस िबल का समथर्न करता हूँ और इसके  िलए कुछ सूचना भी देना 
चाहता हूँ। This Bill aims to bring uniformity, clarity and efficiency in the administration of 
all CAPFs. It seeks to create a common
 framework for recruitment, service 
114
[ RAJYA SABHA ]
114
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

conditions, transfer, promotions and discipline matters. One of the key benefits of this 
Bill is standardization. When all forces fo llow similar rules, it reduces confusion and 
improves coordination between different fo rces. Second, the Bill aims to improve 
personnel management. Clear rules for prom otions, postings and service conditions 
can boost morale among the jawans. A motivated force is always a more effective 
force. Third, the Bill can help in faster  decision-making. When administrative 
processes are streamlined, the delays are re duced. This is very important in security 
forces where quick decision can save liv es. Fourth, it can strengthen accountability 
and transparency. A clear administrative syst em ensures that rules are applied fairly 
and consistently. However, while supporting the intent of the Bill, we must also 
carefully look at some important concerns. First is welfare of personnel. CAPF jawans  
work in very difficult conditions, extreme weather, high risk areas, long working hours 
and separation from families. Any administra tive reform must focus strongly on their 
welfare, mental health, housing and family  support. Second is work-life balance. 
Many personnel face stress due to continuo us deployment. This Bill should ensure 
proper leave policy, rotation system an d rest period. Third is training and 
modernisation. Along with administrative  reforms, we must invest in modern 
equipment, technology and regular training. Fourth is grievance redressal. The jawans 
should have a strong and fair system to raise their concerns without fear. Fifth is 
coordination with States. Since CAPFs often work with State police, clear 
coordination mechanisms are necessary. Sir, we must remember that behind every 
uniform, there is a human being, a son, a daughter, a parent, who has chosen to 
serve the nation. This Bill is not just abou t administration, it is about making our 
forces stronger, more efficient and more humane. If it is implemented properly, this 
Bill can improve efficiency, boost morale and strengthen national security.  
Sir, I would also like to highlight the experience of Telangan a in strengthening 
its security systems. Under the leadership of Shri K. Chandrashekar Rao, Telangana 
has taken several proactive steps to modernize its police and security framework. The 
State Government has provided new vehi cles, advanced weapons, surveillance 
cameras and modern monitoring systems to improve policing and security. These 
measures have, significantly, strength ened real-time surveillance and response 
capabilities. Special focus has also been given to tackling Naxal- related challenges. 
Through better intelligence systems, technol ogy use, and coordinated operations in 
forest areas, Telangana has been able to  control extremist activities effectively. 
Earlier, certain regions faced serious Naxa l issues. Today, the situation has improved 
considerably. This reflects how investme nt in technology, infrastructure, and 
[ 30 March, 2026 ]
115

personnel support can transform security  outcomes. This experience offers an 
important lesson, but at the same time, we must ensure that reforms do not become 
rigid or insensitive to ground reality.  In conclusion, I would say that this Bill is a step 
in the right direction. With some careful im provements and strong focus on personnel 
welfare, it can truly transform the function ing of our Central Armed Police Forces. Let 
us work together to support our forces not just through words, but with effective 
policies because a strong nation depends on strong and well-supported security 
forces.

129

Need for installation of more Doppler Weather Radars in the North-East Region 
SHRI MAHARAJA SANAJAOBA LEISHEMBA (Manipur) : Sir, due to its complex 
mountainous terrain, heavy monsoon rainfall, frequent flash floods, landslides, 
thunderstorms, cloudbursts and increasing climate induced extreme weather events, 
the North-East region requires a far strong er and denser weather monitoring system 
than what presently exists. Here are some  reasons for additional Doppler Weather 
Radars (DWRs) in the region. Firstly, improved forecasting and early warning -- 
Doppler Radars can accurately track precipi tation patterns, detect thunderstorms, 
and provide early warnings for floods, landslides, and cyclones. Second is, 
strengthening Disaster Management.  With better weather forecasting, authorities can 
evacuate people, prepare emergency services, and minimize damage to 
infrastructure and agriculture. Third is, agricultural benefits. Accurate weather 
forecasts can help farmers to plan their acti vities, reducing crop losses and improving 
yields. Fourth is, flood and landslide risk reduction. North East India is prone to 
floods, and Doppler Radars can help mo nitor rainfall patterns, enabling timely 
interventions. Fifth is, climate change mi tigation. As climate change increases 
extreme weather events, Doppler Radars can help to monitor and predict these 
changes. 
 Currently, there are limited Doppler Rada r installations in the North East. In 
Assam, these radars are installed at Guwa hati, Dhubri, Jorhat, Tezpur, and Silchar. 
In Mizoram at Aizawl, in Nagaland at Dimapur, in Manipur at Imphal, and in Arunachal 
Pradesh at Mandala Top and Namsai. Adding  more DWRs can significantly improve 
weather forecasting and disaster management in the region. I, therefore, urge the 
Government to set up more Doppler Weather Radars in the North East Region.                                   
THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (DR. DINESH SHARMA) : The following hon’ble Members

SHRI NIRANJAN (Odisha) : Hon’ble Vice-Chairman, Sir, thank you for giving me an

उपसभाध्यक्ष (डा. िदनेश शमार्) : आपको जो पेपर िदया गया है, उसे पढ़ कर बोिलए।  
Demand for strict implementation of the Supreme Court guidelines for  
the cancellation of fake caste certificates 
SHRI NIRANJAN BISHI (Odisha) : Sir, I seek to draw attention of the Government to 
the serious injustice faced by genuine Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes due to 
the misuse of fake caste certificates by inel igible persons. Despite clear directions of 
the hon’ble Supreme Court of India rega rding verification and cancellation of 
fraudulent caste certificates, many non-SC /ST individuals continue to obtain such 
certificates and illegally avail benefits mean t exclusively for the most marginalized 
communities. As a result of which, they secure Government jobs under reserved 
quotas, obtain admission to professional courses such as MBBS and Engineering, 
purchase protected SC/ST land, and recei ve scholarships, subsidies and other 
welfare benefits meant for genuine beneficiaries. This directly violates the 
constitutional safeguards provided under Articles 15(4), 16(4), 46, 338 and 338A of 
the Constitution of India, which mand ate the protection and advancement of 
Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes.  
132
[ RAJYA SABHA ]
132
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

Once a caste certificate is found to be fraudulent by the competent Scrutiny 
Committee, strict action must follow. Such certificates must be cancelled, and 
persons who secured Government employment  on false claims should be removed 
from service. The benefits illegally availed in  education, stipends, subsidies and loans 
should also be recovered through appropri ate legal proceedings. Another concern is 
that many offenders obtain st ay orders from High Courts and continue to enjoy these 
benefits for years. Therefore, I urge the Government to ensure time-bound disposal of 
such cases. Protecting the rights of genuine SC and ST communities is both a 
constitutional duty and a moral responsibility of the State. Thank you, Sir.

Concern over urban flooding and need for sustainable planning in Gurugram 
SHRIMATI REKHA SHARMA (Haryana): Vice-Chairman, Sir, I seek to draw attention 
of the Government to the issue of urban floo ding and infrastructure challenges in the 
rapidly growing city of Gurugram. Guru gram has emerged as one of the most 
important economic and technological ce ntres in the country. The city hosts 
numerous multinational companies and ha s become a major hub for business, 
innovation and employment generation. I would like to commend the Government for 
its efforts to strengthen infrastructure pl anning across the country through initiatives 
such as the PM Gati Shakti National Master  Plan. However, due to rapid urbanization 
and population growth, Gurugram occasiona lly faces challenges related to water 
drainage and urban flooding during th e monsoon season. Addressing these 
challenges requires coordinated planning involving improved drainage systems, better 
urban design, stronger storm-water in frastructure and the use of modern 
technologies for city planning. Strengthening urban infrastructure will not only improve 
the quality of life for citizens, but will also  help sustain Gurugram’s role as a major 
economic hub.  I urge the Government to look into it.

[ 30 March, 2026 ]

PUBLISHED UNDER RULE 260 OF RULES OF PROCEDURE AND CONDUCT OF
